The Restaurants Already on Eater Editors’ Radar for 2027

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

These restaurants are already creating buzz

Editors’ scouting for the 2026 Eater Awards, presented by Capital One, wrapped up at the end of August — just as fall restaurant-opening season was heating up. That means several notable restaurants that debuted in 2026 didn’t quite make the cut, either because they didn’t open in time or had only been open a matter of weeks and were still finding their footing as of our deadline. But that just gives us a place to start on next year’s research. Here are some early contenders on our radar — places we’re thrilled to have in the conversation no matter how the scene shakes up as 2026 wraps and we continue into 2027.

Our New York team is excited to see the full spectrum of what pastry chef Camari Mick has in store with her own new venture, Afro-Caribbean brasserie and bakery Calabash, which opened in the West Village in early October.

Also in the West Village, the Taqueria Ramírez team debuted this Mexican-seafood-focused wine bar, which translates to “the hairy shrimp.” The tiny convivial space opened in mid-September.

Alex McCoy’s resurrected Thai restaurant, which opened in early September, is dishing up some of the spiciest Thai food the D.C. area has seen since the much-lamented closure of Little Serow. The alleyway hot spot in Georgetown is drawing crowds for its monkfish curry, khao soi, and natural wine list. Thanks to its moody, candlelit atmosphere, it’s also becoming a favorite tucked-away date spot.

Chicago’s tavern-style pizza has been having a moment outside of the Midwest; in D.C., it’s a menu centerpiece of La Merna, which has been one of the city’s tough-to-get tables since its August opening. Co-owner Bradley Shorten used to run a few tavern-style pizzerias in Chicago, and his wife and partner Meg Schmitt, who has a background in cheese distribution, is topping pastas with cheeses she makes fresh. The wood-paneled dining room with red leather-covered booths resembles a Midwest supper club with its laid-back, old-school atmosphere.

We had high hopes that this coastal Italian Pittsburgh restaurant — from the team behind Lilith, Jamilka Borges and Dianne DeStefano — would open in time for 2026 Eater Awards consideration, but the opening date kept continually getting pushed back. The most recent estimate is “late fall.”

Atlanta already knows the team behind Lucian can combine a fantastic wine list with thoughtful food and a carefully curated retail experience; they’ve started doing it again at Sargent, the combination bistro and bookstore that hadn’t yet opened when our scouts hit the city in early August. The classic menu and gorgeous space are already catching attention in the city; we’re also keeping our eyes on the upcoming opening of Thai-style bar Some Luck from the Talat Market team, slated to open any day now.

This new modern Mexican restaurant was only a few days old when our correspondent Matthew Kang stopped by in early September. Our LA team has high standards for tacos, and Kang says the top-notch Chihuahuan food here could already hold its own even when competing with standout California options. Kang appreciated Chiwas’s delicious seafood and short ribs, and approachable, homey vibe; we’ll be back to see how the restaurant matures.

October brought the debut of this ambitious restaurant from chef Jonathan Granada, and editor Dianne de Guzman is ready to try the tableside duck, laminated brioche, and a gougeres-like Parmesan tart — all set inside an Art Deco-style former post office.

We thought this long-in-the-works, highly anticipated Filipino cocktail bar might open in time for us to visit Detroit and check it out this year, but the project from Tyler Olivier and Marcee Sobredilla is still wrapping up construction.

This Evanston, Illinois, project is the first full-service restaurant from Chicago barbecue royalty D’Andre Carter and Heather Bublick. The restaurant is a true celebration of their community, decked out with local art and paired with a steakhouse-esque menu. It opened just outside our 2026 cutoff, and it’s looking like an exciting time for barbecue in the Windy City in general, as Chicago also awaits the opening of Sanders BBQ Prime in Hyde Park.

Lielle was a hit with the Eater LA team when it opened this year, and our team might even be more enamored with follow-up Marée, which just missed our window with its early September opening. At a recent visit, Matthew Kang noted the creamy mussels au gratin and Instagram-worthy squab, and says the au poivre burger earns its $42 price tag.

The 2026 Chefs of the Year

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Five U.S. chefs making waves in their communities with compelling menus

If you buy something from a link, Vox Media may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Each of the chefs featured in our new national Chef of the Year category made news in a major way. But their most recent projects are just one part of their story.

This year we celebrate five chefs who are making an impact both on the city where they cook and on the larger culinary conversation. — Missy Frederick, editorial director

Learn more about this year’s Eater Awards, presented by Capital One, and how editors selected them, here.

San Francisco diners became acquainted with powerhouse couple Laura and Sayat Ozyilmaz through their championing of Eastern Mediterranean cuisine at Dalida. That restaurant told the story of Sayat’s upbringing and influences in Istanbul, Turkey, opening in 2023 inside a historic building within the lush environs of the Presidio and earning critical acclaim for the duo’s cooking.

In the years since, diners came to know Laura outside of Dalida. She completed an impressive run on Top Chef in 2024, where viewers saw her deftly plant one foot in Mediterranean food and another in Mexican cuisine, showcasing her skill in both fields throughout the season. With the 2026 debut of the Ozyilmazes’ restaurant Maria Isabel, the food feels like an even fuller expression of Laura’s heritage and personal history.

Named after Laura’s sister (Maria) and her mother (Isabel), the restaurant steeps in Mexican matriarchy, highlighting female winemakers, ceramics made by Mexican producers, and indigenous Mexican ingredients. The menu channels Laura’s upbringing in Guerrero and her family’s Sinaloan roots, as recounted through mariscos and maiz, moles and salsa macha. The ceviche Acapulqueño reimagines the Fanta- and ketchup-laden version instead with marigold flowers and preserved Seville oranges dressing tender shrimp and raw scallop; petals — and three fried shrimp heads — adorn the top. Duck enmoladas showcase the power of mole negro against a seasonal mole (sometimes a peach manchamanteles, other times a rhubarb rendition, but always compelling). Chochoyotes, a lesser-seen dish in San Francisco, demonstrates the restaurant’s ability to apply masa in different ways — in this case, dumplings — while harnessing the earthy flavor of huitlacoche. Maria Isabel is both a conduit for diners to connect with complex Mexican flavors and a view into Laura’s vision of the Mexico of her past and future. — Dianne de Guzman, editor, Eater SF

Chef Carlos Delgado’s meteoric rise in D.C. began in 2022 with the opening of tiny tasting room Causa, located in Shaw, where he sends diners on a seafaring voyage along the Peruvian coastline and into the Andes he grew up around, and its accompanying upstairs pisco destination Bar Amazonia. His meticulous sourcing and culinary skills quickly drew critical acclaim.

2026 might be the Lima native’s biggest year yet: His breakout project, Maru San, commanded instant Capitol Hill buzz upon opening in February for its orchestra of Nikkei-style hand rolls, sashimi, and other polished seafood plates he and fellow José Andrés alum Simon Lam pass across a 25-seat black marble counter under a big pink octopus mural, as his favorite tropical house blasts in the background. A rare few get to sample the restaurant’s elaborate tasting menu full of ingredients like high-end uni and dry-aged fish; reservations for the 15-course feast are nearly impossible to get.

The ambitious chef, at 36, isn’t afraid to stretch in many directions. He linked up with the Eaternity Group last year to give the Mediterranean-themed La Vie a needed menu makeover at D.C.’s Wharf development — meanwhile, at least one more Maru San is in the works elsewhere in D.C.

Sounds a lot like his former mentor: Before Causa, Delgado honed his craft under José Andrés as the longtime executive chef at Penn Quarter’s Peruvian mainstay China Chilcano. They still text frequently. — Tierney Plumb, Northeast editor

While Amir Hajimaleki has had American-leaning restaurants in Austin for well over a decade, he hasn’t had the opportunity to fully express himself as an Iranian-born, Texas-raised chef. That is, until he opened Roya, his most personal restaurant and a project that has been in the works for more than eight years. Here, he manifests the depth and diversity of Persian cuisine, a bold feat for the middle of Texas. The space, designed with his brother Ali Hajimaleki, feels transportive, with intricate tile mosaics, burnished gold and dark blue hues, and delicate chandeliers.

There’s been an electric energy to the Philly restaurant scene in recent years. The city, ever the underdog, has long punched above its weight; now, the rest of the world has come to the same realization. Amid this buzz, two newer restaurants have outrun the shadow cast by the city’s big names: Cambodian noodle house Mawn, which opened in 2023, and Southeast Asian oyster house and raw bar Sao, which opened last September.

At their helm is the slick, stylish Phila Lorn. The restaurants he runs with his wife Rachel feel singular, blending the influences of his Philadelphia upbringing with his Cambodian background in food that feels playfully free and emboldened by a lack of boundaries between cuisines. At Mawn, funnel cake meets coconut macaroons, and vinegar peppers enhance scallops in red curry. At Sao, the crispy rice in the nam khao salad, which features Italian sausage, feels analogous to arancini. Tuna carpaccio is served with crispy shallots and jalapeños, then cured in lime until it picks up the aura of the bowls of beef pho on Washington Avenue.

Hear Lorn talk, with his South Philly-bred bravado (and requisite uses of “jawn”) giving him a commanding presence, and it’s clear that the city’s culinary community is listening, rallying, and getting stoked to eat more of his food. — Bettina Makalintal, senior reporter

Tucson-based chef Aaron Lopez had a vision that started in his small California city of El Centro, an agricultural city near the U.S.-Mexico border. There, he hatched a plan to create a menu imbued with the techniques and flavors of the Sonoran Desert that spans northern Mexico, Arizona, and southeastern California. Trained at top kitchens in Los Angeles and beyond, Lopez researched by going deep into the desert, armed with a camera and his wits. A year later, he and his wife June Chee carried the project to Tucson, a larger venue still deeply embedded in the desert, but with a new audience.

Inside the high-ceilinged, brick-walled downtown space, Ursa serves as a hushed gallery for Lopez and his team’s $150 tasting menu, which merges Indigenous cooking techniques with foraged and locally farmed ingredients. Barrel fruit (a yellow cactus) topped with the tang of pickled chiltepin leaves is the first bite, a luscious treat you may never see on a European plate. Quail breast arrives on a metal skewer dressed in pickled yucca, hovering over a jujube broth like a quiet offering. Goat roasted in agave, pit barbacoa-style, is unveiled tableside and served over softened tepary beans.

At every turn, with no extraneous adornments, the kitchen lets the produce and meats boast their innate nature. Lopez eschews conventional comfort, reimagining a meal of cactus, corn, sunflower tea, and beans as true luxuries, and the results are evocative and powerful. His message — seeing the value and beauty of this landscape, its people, its ingredients, and the way it all comes through the cooking — is the luscious pith of the cactus you find beneath the surface of the American Southwest. — Matthew Kang, correspondent

The 2026 Restaurant Dishes of the Year

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

From an earthy curry crab callaloo dip to a plate of pickled walleye cheeks, these were editors’ most memorable bites

Our editors ate in hundreds of restaurants across the country this year, but certain dishes left a lasting impression, and we’re recognizing them in this new national Eater Award category.

From a textbook execution of the ubiquitous 2026 entree — roast chicken — to an innovative fish preparation offering Midwestern diners a new way to experience a familiar ingredient, these dishes are all worth celebrating. — Missy Frederick, editorial director

Learn more about this year’s Eater Awards, presented by Capital One, and how editors selected them, here.

When experiencing a five-course tasting menu ($145), I wasn’t expecting toast to be a main attraction. But even with competition from luxurious courses featuring everything from a complex Hokkaido scallop to an assertive charred rib-eye with peaches, All Well’s puffed grain toast left a lasting impression.

After three years at two-Michelin-starred Oriole, Larry Feldmeier partnered with Noah Sandoval, fellow Oriole alum and James Beard winner for Best Chef: Great Lakes, to open the ambitious West Loop restaurant All Well, which offers both a la carte bar options and the aforementioned tasting; the toast is only available as part of the latter experience.

The dish arrives to the table amplified with nutty buckwheat butter sweetened with honey, the backdrop for a pasta stuffed with triple-cream Délice de Bourgogne and a cauldron of salty chicken brodo. You’ll dive into the pasta, of course, using the bread to sop up the rich beurre blanc. Like a performer waiting in the wings for their monologue, the puffed grain toast takes center stage. The toast effectively offsets the creaminess and saltiness of the rest of the dish, but can stand on its own even without the accompanying pasta. Its subtle sweetness almost acts as a mid-meal dessert, a distinctive break in a seamless dinner you don’t quite want to end. — Isaiah Reynolds, audience editor

The only thing about Oklahoma City that feels even vaguely reminiscent of the Caribbean is the sheet-pan-flat horizon. And yet, inside a gaping industrial dining room downtown, there’s Dougla Kitchen, where diners sip fruity rum drinks out of plastic bags, and acclaimed chef Andrew Black digs deep in his past to bring OKC locals a taste of his Indo-Jamaican upbringing. I fully admit this Midwestern-born, California-raised girl doesn’t encounter ruddy callaloo greens as often as she’d like (my hunch is neither do most Oklahomans). No matter. What Black manages to do with this deceiving pile of greenish-brown mush, dank with hand-picked crab and sparkling with curry spices, translates the soul of Caribbean home cooking in a single scoop. I didn’t know it was possible to be overwhelmed by a sense memory from a life that’s not your own, but this heady appetizer does that, served with a side of crisp taro chips and worth lingering over all afternoon with a couple of Red Stripes. — Lesley Suter, brand director

To understand California cuisine in 2026, look no further than Henrietta, a neighborhood bistro that has struck a just-right balance of precision and ingenuity while imbuing a feeling of nostalgia that begs locals and travelers alike to return. The restaurant’s intentions come across most clearly in its roast chicken, served as a just-salty-enough half chicken hunked into handheld pieces with deep, smoky char marks. The prize chicken pile gets tucked onto a plate alongside a scorched lemon half and a heaping, chicory-heavy Caesar panzanella blanketed under a layer of Parmesan that could be a snowcap on a stretch of the San Gabriel Mountains. With the chicken, Henrietta nods to forebears (namely San Francisco’s Zuni), while making the dish its own with a Baja-born Caesar salad that feels distinctly Los Angeles, dotted with torn fistfuls of bread from the neighboring local Clark Street Bakery. Of course, 2026 continues to be the year of the Great Chickening, where roast chicken has become many a restaurant’s calling card — and an easy way into the cultural cache. In Los Angeles alone, Sqirl (with its new dinner menu) and Pasjoli have both put out progressive, near-intoxicating spins on the dish, but Henrietta’s has lodged itself into the collective imagination with its brazen simplicity. — Rebecca Roland, deputy editor, Eater Southern California/Southwest

The striking cantaloupe and verjus terrine at Nashville’s Junior offers a glimpse into the minds of chef Philip Krajeck and chef de cuisine Brian Mejia, the duo behind the high-technique kitchen. A “terrine” slice arrives with pieces of cantaloupe suspended in a clear gelatin made with verjus, the pressed juice of unripened grapes. One corner is covered in tiny, diced cubes of cantaloupe dressed in citrus-forward turmeric oil; another gets draped in chamomile-steeped cream. The mix of textures and flavors comes together in a dynamic symphony: the bright, acidic cubes of summer-ripened fruit against the calming mellowness of chamomile and gelatin layers that meld cantaloupe juice sweetness with crisp verjus.

The dessert’s structural elegance, which highlights a French variety of melon grown by a local farmer, may not immediately reveal its full complexity: Clear gelatin features a layer of clarified cantaloupe juice with encased melon slices, stacked between two layers of verjus gelatin. The melon gets compressed to intensify its flavors. Somehow, Junior managed to make Jell-O cool again — and dare I say, even sophisticated? — an example of Junior’s steady confidence that makes the restaurant an irresistible experience. — Dianne de Guzman, lead editor, Eater SF

Though people outside the Midwest may not be familiar with freshwater whitefish walleye, there it’s a classic comfort food, a staple of Lenten fish fries, state fair booths, and Lake Erie fishermen’s freezer stashes. At Rosy, chef Vinnie Cimino leans on that familiarity to help convince his customers to experience a beloved ingredient in an entirely new way. Pickled walleye cheeks ($17) have been a recurring staple — if a fleeting one — on Rosy’s Eastern European-leaning menu since the restaurant opened in February 2026. Kate’s Fish in Cleveland’s historic West Side Market provides walleye filets for sister restaurant Cordelia; when breaking down and fileting each fish, the fishmongers save the cheeks until they have a formidable stash. About every three weeks, Rosy can offer pickled walleye cheeks for around four days at a time. It’s a thoughtful move for sustainability, but also for succulence, as fish cheeks are a prized cut for their luscious texture.

Cimino conceived the dish as a nod to Jewish fish-pickling practices (for me, the results also brought to mind a tender ceviche). Seasonal vegetables are pickled with lemon — an earlier version invoked German-style potato salad, spring brought asparagus, and, when I went in June, bell pepper, carrot, and dill were in the mix. They’re then combined with the cheeks, which get a separate hot-pickling treatment involving red wine vinegar, salt, sugar, bay leaf, peppercorns, and coriander. “People really gravitate toward it,” Cimino says. “We’ve built up a great trust with our diners.” While the dish won’t be replacing fried walleye at area fish fries anytime soon, Cimino feels proud that when at Rosy, diners have been more than willing to go along with him for the ride. — MF

The 2026 Best New Bakeries in America

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Precision pastries are sweeping the nation. These bakers are leading the charge.

If there’s one thing we’ve noticed over the past several years, it’s how much bakeries have stepped up their game.

Some embody a personal expression of a baker’s identity, while others push the envelope beyond expected flavor combinations; all excel at a time when “little treat” culture is at its peak. — Missy Frederick, editorial director

Learn more about this year’s Eater Awards, presented by Capital One, and how editors selected them, here.

If one new place in Chicago needs no further hype, it is Bad Butter. You’ll know you’re there when you spot the line cascading out of the whitewashed storefront along West Cortland Street, a queue that doesn’t let up until the pastry case is empty. Formerly a head baker at Soho House Chicago, Dan Koester was stuck at home like the rest of us in 2020, handing off excess home-baked sourdough loaves to friends and family and making some available for free pickup. After he was officially laid off that summer, he launched his own online bakery business, earning a massive following along the way.

One residency at the Emily Hotel later, Koester debuted his Bucktown bakery, demonstrating the full range of his skill. Still warm, charred, and dimpled flats of focaccia showered with scallion furikake rest on its shelves. Impossibly crisp and exquisitely laminated twice-baked almond croissants burst with subtle, aromatic almond paste. A spicy harissa monkey bread with a cloudlike texture is cooled by a pool of creamy labneh drizzled with silky olive oil.

The bakery’s menu has breadth and also manages to arc with the season. Customers snake along the floor waiting their turn to claim a towering cruffin, dusted in fine grains of sugar and piped full of ginger plum jam, or a seasonal tart piled with plump, lacquered blueberries. A pastry box filled with these confections is well worth the wait. — Brenna Houck, dining editorial manager

Born as an underground dinner party operated out of Karla Subero Pittol’s Echo Park garage, Chainsaw finally got its own digs in November 2025 in the form of a lively cafe in Melrose Hill, an unquestionably gentrifying neighborhood that has become home to some of Los Angeles’s best independent restaurants. At the cafe, Subero Pittol channels her Venezuelan heritage through a menu of arepas plump with beans, limey guasacaca, and cheese; a Venezuelan hot dog (“perro caliente”) buoyed by an acidic slaw; and her bracingly tart passionfruit-lime icebox pie, which commands a cult following all its own.

Stepping into Chainsaw feels like you could be in Subero Pittol’s own kitchen as she greets regulars and newcomers as if they are old friends. Customers study a handwritten menu that changes daily — it doesn’t help to come in too attached to any one dish (save the ever-present icebox pie) when you may get tempted by another. The cafe’s impact on Los Angeles feels clear in the near-constant crowds that hover outside, reading their newspapers over an espresso or tucking into the latest of Subero Pittol’s ideas, such as a full English breakfast served for a single day. In a city lacking in untamed Venezuelan cooking, Chainsaw offers a home for Subero Pittol to celebrate the cuisine in a way that feels distinctly Los Angeles. — Rebecca Roland, deputy editor, Eater Southern California/Southwest

With both Afghan and Latin American influences, Diljān gives New Yorkers a bakery with a point of view and innumerable reasons to become a regular. Ali Zaman and Mohamed Ghiasi, owners of Astoria’s Little Flower Cafe, teamed up with Bryan Ford, Honduran American baker and cookbook author, to conceptualize the bakery, now perched on a corner near Atlantic Avenue in Brooklyn Heights.

Their combined talent makes for some of the city’s most compelling baked goods. For the Saffron Shah, laminated pastry holds delicate saffron cream in the shape of a crescent, an important Islamic symbol. Halwa tops the sticky bun, while a swirl of samosa pastry wraps halal lamb under a dusting of powdered sugar. Don’t miss the enormous Afghan flatbread crowned by toppings such as halal beef bacon with scallions or sour cherry cream cheese. The team has also branched into pizzas topped with cheese and Afghan cumin, kofta kebabs, or beef pepperoni. Look for specials like the flan with cardamom-poached apricots and conchas stuffed with firni, a rice pudding. — Nadia Chaudhury, deputy editor, Eater New York

San Francisco’s pastry game is stronger and more competitive than ever before, and Parachute Bakery steps to the forefront of a crowded field with a mix of patisserie standards and seasonal standouts. At his Ferry Building bakery, pastry chef Nasir Armar marries his fine dining training, honed at Michelin-starred Sorrel and Saison, with flavors that make everyone sit up and take notice (and snap a food photo or two for their socials).

Vegetables and savory complements are given their own footing on the menu. Stalks of grilled asparagus shine in a baton-shaped croissant shell, with fennel jam, sheep’s milk ricotta, and ramp pesto alongside them; one brief, bordering-on-gimmicky croissant got piped with clam chowder, a reference to the soup in a bread bowl famously served on the city’s wharf. Even potato-laden samosas and bright tomato shakshuka get interpreted into pastry form. Parachute succeeds with its experimental vision, a hype well-earned that leaves fans clamoring for what they’ll come up with next. — Dianne de Guzman, editor, Eater SF

The Best New Restaurants in America in 2026

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The year’s top restaurant debuts give us infinite reasons to dine out right now

These are the 15 new restaurants that best captured the year in dining. Our team of editors and scouts traveled across the country, drawing on years of experience to build the 2026 winners list.

What we found remarkable: the quiet confidence of a chef who’s mastered Hungarian food in Pittsburgh; a fresh revamp of family-style Italian dining in Philadelphia grounded in the original restaurant’s 1950s roots; a cozy tribute to Southern cuisine in Denver that still manages to feel cool. What they have in common: a sincere commitment to making diners feel at home. — Missy Frederick, editorial director

Learn more about this year’s Eater Awards, presented by Capital One, and how editors selected them, here.

“You went to Kansas City for sushi?” I was asked more than once — with some derision — after returning from Missouri, so I can only imagine the uphill battle chef Peter Hoang faces at Akoya Omakase. Hoang isn’t easily daunted. He’s so committed to sourcing, he often personally drives to the airport to pick up the fish hand-selected by his two private buyers in Tokyo 26 hours after placing his order.

At Akoya, the fish quality is unassailable; the rice seasoned with precision. But Hoang consistently finds ways to complement Akoya’s sashimi and nigiri with unexpected accents. Smoky juvenile king mackerel gets a hit of acid and sweetness from pineapple chutney. Truffle, notorious for being overwhelming on just about anything, manifests in pate form alongside dashi and kelp salt to highlight delicate hirame. The miso-butter-accented crab nigiri (a steal at $6) calls to mind decadently dipping crab legs in drawn butter. While affordable for omakase, $165 is still a commitment: Akoya diners have the option to test the waters with a la carte offerings and lunch specials in a warm and unpretentious restaurant. Befitting the Midwestern stereotype, the personable chefs, who chat freely with those seated across the bar, are eager to answer questions. Even tables in the dining room chime in.

Hoang, who worked in his father’s mid-2000s sushi restaurant before heading to bigger markets like New York and Chicago, is excited to be part of his hometown’s emerging Japanese dining scene — one that’s increasingly drawing national attention thanks to ambitious restaurants like Anjin izakaya and promising newcomers like day-to-night Tian Tea House/Moon Bar. Kansas City just might develop a reputation for cuisine beyond barbecue. — MF

Assuming new ownership of beloved, old restaurants can be a risk, but South Philly local Joey Baldino has the formula down: Keep the trappings, dial in the food. After taking over his family’s private Palizzi Social Club and retaining its members-only allure, Baldino bought Bomb Bomb Bar, which first opened in 1951, and reopened it last year. The bar’s lore precedes it: A rival business bombed it twice, immortalized by the large neon sign of two cartoonish bombs. Some reboots feel ironic, but not this one; the payphone by the door, the swordfish on the wall, and even the endearingly gruff servers feel like they’ve been there forever. It’s proof that classic Philly and modern culinary destination Philly can coexist.

In the front room, locals analyze Eagles games, and you might hear children playing in the street. In the back dining room, the procession of seafood here, priced at $65 per person, feels like a steal in this era of rising prices. The meat for the lobster Française has been removed from its shell, battered and fried, placed onto a buttery sauce, and then crowned with its shell once more. The crispy calamari gets enlivened with twangy cherry peppers. But it’s the overall dining experience that’s unbeatable: The restaurant hasn’t reinvented the wheel, but as the Beach Boys play and you sip a pepperoncini martini while watching your server pour hot fudge onto a sundae, you remember (perhaps while still wearing a bib) that complete novelty isn’t always necessary. — Bettina Makalintal, senior reporter

Cassis is big in every sense. The nearly 200-seat dining room of the Milwaukee restaurant is an immaculately designed jewel box of gleaming tile, cozy banquettes, and antique mirrors, and even on a Wednesday night, it’s packed and roaring, with a towering, glowing bar at the center of the action. The latest project from Kyle and Meghan Knall, the couple behind nearby acclaimed bistro Birch, channels the conviviality and polish of a classic, old-world French brasserie while remaining distinctly Midwestern in spirit. It’s an all-day restaurant with both heart and panache.

Kyle Knall’s cooking roots itself in French tradition without feeling stodgy or like Parisian cosplay. Instead, it takes cues from his farm-to-table approach at Birch: Classical technique provides a framework for showing off Wisconsin ingredients with playfulness, vividness, and attention to detail. Oeuf mayo gets a jolt from intensely herbaceous, bright green aioli; ravioli Dauphiné pairs almost sugary-sweet summer corn with buttery Wisconsin-made Pleasant Ridge Reserve cheese; and an understated, frill-free but deeply umami foie gras eclair transforms one of French cuisine’s richest ingredients into an almost weightless bite.

At a moment when ambitious dining often means intimate tasting-menu counters and an air of exclusivity, Cassis makes a case that dinner can still feel like a real night out on the town. The cooking has an eagerness to please that succeeds. Within its walls, you’re offered an increasingly rare pleasure: a space where you’re invited to settle in, linger, order another bottle of wine, and maybe even have a friendly chat with the table next to you. Cassis could exist — and surely thrive — in New York or Las Vegas, but it’s more exciting in Milwaukee, where its scale and ambition feel like a vote of confidence in the city itself. — Hilary Pollack, deputy editor, Eater

In 2026, the Midwest has proven a formidable destination for dining, with restaurants from Cleveland to Milwaukee leaning into what makes the region’s food special. Creepies in Chicago embodies this movement: A nonsensical (if memorable) name belies a thoughtful restaurant destination from chefs David and Anna Posey. The refreshing space feels contemporary, but with cottage-esque touches — geometric patterns dot the furniture, cartoonish artwork shares space with kitchenware tchotchkes, and mini curtains hang from open shelving.

Chef de cuisine Tayler Ploshehanski embeds French technique into dishes fiercely grounded in Chicago’s identity. Steamed mussels get kissed with classic Pernod and a fennel giardiniera that honors the city’s iconic Italian beef sandwiches. An Alsatian-leaning tarte flambe comes “tavern-style,” a nod to the square-cut pizza beloved by locals. Not every dish invokes the Great Lakes; the generous portion of gougeres may be Midwest-appropriate, but here the French classic takes a decadent turn as warm Brie seeps from the center of each choux pastry. A textbook roast chicken’s umami-laden sauce gains extra depth from liver; drag one of the restaurant’s hefty, yet impossibly crispy fries through its drippings for the best bite of the night. Save room for Anna Posey’s desserts: The savory soft-serve trend manifests as a baguette-infused concoction topped with chocolate and buttery breadcrumbs. In a year when a diner can grow weary that Everything’s a Bistro these days, Creepies proves some still offer delights and surprises. — MF

I’ve always felt a connection to Cuban cuisine, having visited the island country in my youth and eaten many pan con lechons from LA’s Porto’s Bakery in iconic Glendale. But a July meal at Emelina, which snagged a Michelin star months after opening for its ambitious, $275 Cuban tasting menu, showed me the country’s cooking in an entirely new context.

I wasn’t the only one to find it a revelation. Several older Cubans fortunate enough to nab a reservation got emotional toward the end of the meal, moved to tears as they told me they’d never had Cuban food like this before.

Located in tony West Palm Beach, this striking restaurant from chefs Osmel González and Camila Salazar draws inspiration from the former’s family history and upbringing in Cuba. After working together at world-class restaurants like Disfrutar in Spain and SingleThread in California, they partnered with Alvaro Perez-Miranda to achieve their vision. Employing lush ingredients sourced from South Florida farms in Redland and Lake Worth, the meal starts with a tender knob of fried flounder topped with caviar — a winking nod to traditional pan con minuta, a fried fish sandwich slathered with tartar sauce. A mid-course of pristine tropical fruits including mamey, lychee, and mango, arranged over creamy ricotta and dotted with Benton’s country ham, conjures a bounty one might linger over on a Cuban beach. Rabo encendido, or braised oxtail with Koshihikari rice, takes the homestyle guisado to new heights.

Cuba has a long history of home restaurants called paladares, serving personalized versions of staple dishes like arroz con pollo. Emelina represents that same opportunity to delight and enlighten, casting attention on a cuisine and culture that could have been. In balmy West Palm Beach, it thrives. — Matthew Kang, correspondent

The winding walkway into Maroon is a portal into a new dimension, a leap from the beeps and bloops of the casino floor at the Sahara Las Vegas into a thrumming dining room, dimly lit and streaked with red-orange accents. Chef Kwame Onwuachi, who most recently earned acclaim at Tatiana in New York City and Dōgon in Washington, D.C., has become a beacon of African diasporic dishes in the middle of the Mojave Desert.

Onwuachi is building something approachable yet grounded in technique. The name Maroon comes from 17th- and 18th-century enslaved people who escaped and established mountain communities in Jamaica; the kitchen centers African and Caribbean flavors rooted in their foodways. Consider the artful array of bluefin tuna belly chunks, hamachi, and braised octopus, swimming in a tangy broth pool and best enjoyed spooned onto fried saltine crackers, that gets an intense but brief spice hit from Scotch bonnet peppers. Luxurious, palm-sized oxtail patties and jerk mushroom earn a dash of caviar and grated truffle, a maximalist garnish on the typically humble dish. Grilled butterflied shrimp brushed with achiote sofrito recall pescado zarandeado, while sweet white grape chow chow brings a puckery kick. The blackened jerk chicken, its juiciness sealed in with a crust of aromatic spices, might be the finest in the U.S.; oxtail fried rice could moonlight as Caribbean bibimbap, with saucy deboned beef and fork-tender stewed chicken on an assemblage of julienned peppers and peas. Yes, there is steak, but go for the seared, bone-in pork chop instead for its sweet glaze.

Maroon engages you with sensory brilliance and familiar yet indelible flavors, like a catchy song that gets better with each listen. In a different city, it might feel overwrought, but here in Las Vegas, it matches the city’s frenetic energy and lands like a satisfying opus. — MK

Maydan Market opened in 2025 as a collaborative restaurant space and marketplace home to a few different Los Angeles-born businesses; in essence, it functions as one super-incubator for some of the best food you can find in the city. Diners bounce around to try a pozole flight and pescadillas (sofrito-fried fish nested in supple blue corn tortillas) at Maléna, from the family behind the beloved Tamales Elena; a crisp, UFO-sized, asiento-painted tlayuda from Lugya’h, from Los Angeles legend Poncho’s Tlayudas; and chile-sauce-slicked gai tod from Golden Mountain Chicken (formerly Yhing Yhang), from the intrepid team behind Thai restaurant Holy Basil. They will certainly stop to perch at the rotating Club 104 stall, which has hosted Black-owned businesses such as Melnificent Wingz and Caribbean diasporic pop-up Rubie, as well as Palestinian home restaurant Mid East Eats. On their way out, they may circle the storefront simply named Sook to eye wines, spices, and woven goods sourced from the Levant.

This is not to mention the market’s anchor restaurants: the Lebanese-influenced Maydan and Compass Rose, respectable D.C. transplants from prolific restaurateur Rose Previte, who, alongside her team, curated the market with the knockout lineup of Indigenous vendors. Maydan Market feels boldly like one whole; people rarely come for a single hyped stall. The point, in effect, is the crawl. In a year marked by ongoing, unrelenting, nationwide ICE raids targeting Black and brown immigrants, the fact that the market centers Indigenous Mexican-owned businesses, allowing their owners the space and sovereignty to reach a wider stage and audience, speaks loudly. — Nicole Fellah, dining editorial manager

Take the former location of Portland’s — if not North America’s — most famous Northern Thai restaurant, combine it with the city’s most prolific and highly decorated Thai chef and restaurateur in Akkapong “Earl” Ninsom, and pepper in an elite local bar manager — co-owner Eric Nelson. Mix it all together, and you get OK Chicken & Khao Soi. Those familiar with its predecessor, Pok Pok, will find the space uncannily similar: a covered patio and downstairs service at a small bar, upstairs seating at oilcloth-wrapped tables. Now customers order on paper tabs. It’s casual and intimate, with just the right level of noise to feel like you stumbled into a really good dinner party.

OK Chicken’s menu leans into the flavors of Chiang Mai, starting with crispy chicken wings and two styles of khao soi — one with chicken and another richly prepared with beef. Plates of smoky nam prik makheua (eggplant dip) come with crisp and waxy long beans, green cabbage leaves, and cucumber. A memorable dish of yum moo yang — tender grilled pork shoulder tossed with snappish chunks of celery stalk and leaves as well as sliced onion — relaxes in a funky fish sauce with Thai chiles that sneakily build in your system until your lips hum. An herbal tea slushie refreshes even when mixed with a shot of infused Scotch. Close the meal with longan sticky rice speckled with perilla seeds, showered with crispy coconut and bathed in salted coconut milk that balances the sweetness of the pale lychee fruit and tangy mango ice cream on top. Each bite or sip at OK Chicken is surprising and pleasurable in equal measure. — Brenna Houck, dining editorial manager

In kitchens across the country, chefs have been happily marrying Japanese and Mexican foodways: Both cuisines have a reverence for local grains, honor highly specific seafood preparations, and play on savory, acidic flavors. Ramen Del Barrio blossomed from this shared cultural language — a Japanese Mexican izakaya centering ramen.

Chef and owner Chris Krinsky deftly laces together these threads for dishes such as creamy carnitas tonkotsu with two types of pork, confit buche and belly, as well as a chewy tangle of fresh noodles — made in partnership with Austin chef Gary Wu — using flour from a local mill. A honeycomb tripe and hominy-studded pozole tonkotsu comes rojo- or verde-style, while the mole tsukemen gets paired with thick whole wheat noodles and carnitas for dipping into rich, hearty mole. Tuna tostada, served on top of a sheet of origami paper, packs a zesty punch thanks to a dried shrimp and morita chile aioli, chicharrones furikake, eel sauce, and salsa macha. The yaki-tacos — hefty yakitori skewers served on top of corn tortillas — use smoky pulpo and vegetarian-friendly mushrooms accented with pepitas.

This isn’t fusion for fusion’s sake. Krinsky was born in Maine, grew up in Mexico, and worked at Japanese restaurants throughout most of his career. He started his ramen journey cooking out of his garage and then opened a food court stand, for which he won an Eater Award in 2023. Somehow, his menu tastes better now that he has his own dedicated space where he hand-layered wood panels along the wall and lined the ceiling with sushi mats. A small television in the dining room blasts Spanish-dubbed anime. Ramen Del Barrio 2.0 feels wholly like a loving expression of his culinary fascinations and his commitment to honoring both traditions. — Nadia Chaudhury, deputy editor, Eater New York

In an unmarked space in San Francisco’s Nob Hill, chef Patrick Gabon and partner Celine Wuu quietly rewrote the playbook on fine dining with Restaurant Naides. Filipino food has not historically held a footing in the Bay Area’s higher-end echelons, but Naides’s dishes make a powerful claim that the cuisine deserves its place there. Gabon deploys the sour, salty, and sweet flavors of the Philippines with subtle tenacity: The saline funk of bagoong, or fermented salted shrimp, takes on new dimension in Gabon’s hands, where the condiment gets mellowed into a creamy sauce that acts as foil to tender slices of chayote, crisp duck cracklings, and the burst of caviar. His approach to the mouth-puckering sourness of sinigang invigorates the Philippines’ national dish by pouring dry-aged beef broth over meticulously sliced abalone and scallions. These plates manage to retain the soul of the food but in new forms; they don’t placate the audience, asking them instead to reconsider their fundamental notions of fine dining and how Filipino food presented in this way may upend them.

Wuu’s beverage program has become a force of its own. It’s not often that a nonalcoholic pairing manages to outshine a traditional wine program, but Wuu keenly matches the bold flavors of each dish. A drink mixing the flavors of green mango with rooibos and habañero cordial complements the salty punch of bagoong at one turn, while coconut water, osmanthus, and rose vinegar offer a gentle start with the opening snacks, composed of Gabon’s takes on lumpia, okoy, and puto. “As you age and you grow, you tend to go deeper in your roots: ‘How can I make something so traditional the best it can be?’” Wuu told me earlier this year. Naides has answered that question with every meal since. — Dianne de Guzman, editor, Eater SF

It’s tricky to pin down what exactly Rougarou is. It’s a restaurant, certainly, serving modern Southern food in the historic Five Points neighborhood in Denver. It also has a ripping bar program, late-night hours, live music on the patio, and a casual roguish charm. Inside an understated brick building on a flyby street, the dining room is full of personality, dim and moody, all hard surfaces that bounce around the fun clatter of spirited groups throughout the dinner hour.

The menu conveys the right balance of accessibility and intrigue: The ubiquitous tomato stone fruit salad gets a curry-inflected jolt of vinaigrette and a crunch of coconut benne granola. Charred zucchini is doused in smoked cheddar sauce and scattered with bits of country ham. Fried catfish sits on a pond of fermented hot and sour sauce, with pickled chiles that lash the tongue at first and Sichuan peppercorns that leave you gently tingling. And the signature Chicken on a Stick is a lovingly updated homage to a legendary gas station restaurant in Oxford, Mississippi.

While the food feels approachable, the cocktails are high-concept and assertive, leaning on tropical ingredients but pushing for wilder edges, like green banana, oversteeped oolong, and Haitian clairin, pungent and unctuous with fusel oils. A second visit, late at night, revealed a laid-back restaurant-industry hangout at the bar instead of the celebratory din I found earlier. Much like its namesake monster, Rougarou can shape-shift to host a group dinner, a date night, a jam session, or a beguiling nightcap. It’s the kind of restaurant that invites returns, and a great fit for this moment in restaurant culture. — Ben Mesirow, travel editor

D.C. hospitality veterans Jon Sybert and Jill Tyler took a risk when they converted Adams Morgan’s Michelin-starred favorite Tail Up Goat into Rye Bunny, a restaurant that straddles the line between upscale and casual. Appetizers start at $15 and entree prices go up to $40, and instead of table service, diners line up to order at a counter. In the end, both the owners and D.C. diners have seen a payoff. Sybert’s dishes are inventive yet comforting, with Aleppo honey-covered fried chicken served alongside delicately stacked anchovy and quince jam toasts built on thin slices of milk bread. Everything is still seasonal and sourced from nearby farms, but those high-grade ingredients are easier to afford now that the restaurant has been able to cut down on the number of staff by 20 percent. While D.C. diners are not crazy about queuing, the nightly line out the door has sustained for more than six months and become an unexpected way to meet neighbors. A server brings over a drink menu as you wait, so you can catch up with friends and sip on a Slovenian orange wine before you sit down.

Rye Bunny encompasses the simple joy of a neighborhood restaurant that’s an equally excellent destination for a weekday date night or a special occasion, and it represents a larger shift in our dining landscape that might save these mid-price-range restaurants as margins get thinner. — Emily Venezky, social media manager

When So. Fox opened in Atlanta’s Virginia-Highland neighborhood in July, chef Myles Moody and sommelier Rachael Pack had an instant hit, bringing new energy to that pocket of the city. But a successful restaurant often belies monumental effort behind the scenes. So. Fox’s signature dish — a whole roasted chicken — is only available to five lucky tables per night and regularly sells out before the second seating. Much like a great restaurant takes time to build, the simple bird takes days for its mustard brine to infuse, and the result is stunning: a glistening interior and exquisitely crispy skin.

So. Fox commands an insatiable demand for a table topped with that prized poultry, herby rabbit dumplings, and Carolina Gold rice. Snagging a spot at the welcoming bar or at the curved, floating table near the entry, which helps capture overflow crowds, is equally competitive. The space that formerly housed a burger joint (and before that, an auto repair shop) has been transformed into a bright, perennially packed dining room with grand windows, offering an optimal view of North Highland Avenue.

Short for Southern Fox, an American indigenous grape varietal, So. Fox showcases Pack’s longtime knowledge of natural, low-intervention, biodynamic, and Old World producers, many of whom she knows personally. Pack and Atlanta-born Moody first met while working at New York’s Michelin-starred Aska, and returned south to open Kinship Butcher & Sundry in 2021, which resides next door. This isn’t just farm-to-table; it’s produce from their own Ormewood Park farm, highlighted in grilled beef with Vidalia onions and braised leeks, and at the end of summer, in sugar snap peas with chives and farmer cheese.

It’s seasonal Southern fare that feels current and relevant, with a refreshingly casual approach — and people can’t help but show up for it. — Mona Holmes, editor, Eater LA

Somssi, from Ahris Kim and chef Daniel Gronert, is one of the clearest examples in New York of a restaurant that goes all-in on personal history, rather than focusing on the broader food culture of any one country or a region.

Korea-born Kim, who ran operations at Atomix, lived in Indonesia and the Philippines before moving to Wisconsin with her family. Gronert, who has German roots and spent time as a pitmaster in South Carolina, cooked across restaurants in Atomix’s parent company. Those influences show up in dishes like the caviar-crowned Potato Potato Potato, a crispy-soft tater tot simulacrum nested on miso hollandaise; ox tongue grilled and glazed with tare, a kaleidoscope of textures; and pork schnitzel presented like shrimp toast (it works).

With the team’s fine dining pedigree, Somssi sets a high bar for hospitality, yet it’s still casual: The room is designed to feel like a home, and the menu nods to places Kim and Gronert have lived.

The bar extends that expertise. Christian Gray’s cocktails include well-made minis, which the bartenders are game to explain to curious drinkers. Sommelier (and Punch contributor) Jenny Eagleton’s list of wines and sakes leans toward lesser-known areas. The duo and their staff reward handing your night over to them, whether you’re there with a group or alone with a Kindle.

Personal touches carry through the space in gloriously analog fashion. Lace curtains on the door spell out the restaurant’s name in embroidery, and handprints line the bathroom wall, left by the broader constellation of people who helped build Somssi into the singular restaurant it is today. — NC

Sunday is a very good night to be at Titusz. That’s Schnitzel Sonntag — when the Pittsburgh restaurant, clad in warm wood and gem-toned ceramic tile, offers an irresistible special at an even more irresistible price: a 4-ounce veal cutlet, breaded and fried in lard, for $13. The golden brown slab comes with a single lemon cheek on the plate and nothing else. It takes confidence to serve a dish so simply: Chef and owner Csilla Thackray gained hers in some of the city’s top restaurant kitchens. She named Titusz after her great-grandfather, and his portrait hangs by the first-floor bar. Her own name comes from her Budapest-born grandmother, who raised the chef on her cooking.

Call it grandmacore if you want to peg Titusz to a national trend, but the menu is modern, Austrian, Hungarian, and distinctly Thackray’s. Here, meggyleves, the Magyar sour-cherry soup, gets an electrifying swirl of paprika oil. The chef has made charring an art: You will want to pick up the bowl and drink every drop of charred leek and sauerkraut broth beneath her buoyant kaspressknödel (cheese dumplings). The Maillard reaction pays off again in the savory brown butter drizzled over a dessert of aranygaluska (think walnut monkey bread), served warm on a puddle of peach creme anglaise. Thackray’s husband — Sean Rosenkrans, another veteran of Pittsburgh’s restaurant scene — has built a stunning list of wines from Central Europe, as well as cocktails fluent in that idiom — a slug of herbal Unicum here, a toasty hint of caraway there. Everything makes profound and reassuring sense together. The decision to spend money at a restaurant might feel fraught right now; the choices at Titusz never do. — Beth Kracklauer, dining editorial manager

Review: Metaphor: ReFantazio (Switch 2) - An Extraordinary JRPG That Could Use Another Optimisation Pass

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A game with the name 'Metaphor' that tackles issues such as culture diversity, democracy, and classism isn't exactly subtle in its delivery, but is just as poignant in 2026 as it was when the game first released in 2024.

Atlus has brought its critically acclaimed turn-based role-playing to Switch 2, and in doing so, reminded everyone of two things: That Atlus are absolute masters at delivering striking stories, endearing characters, and engaging combat through the lens of an incredibly unique art style, and that optimisation for handheld consoles is not their strong suit.

Metaphor: ReFantazio is simultaneously the story of a young boy yearning to reconnect with his dear friend, the prince of the kingdom of Euchronia who had been struck with a curse that left him in a comatose state, a story of prejudice and social manipulation through the kingdom itself, started by the assassination of the king at the hands of Louis Guiabarn, a common-born army officer wanting to challenge the status quo through true meritocracy, AND the story of the parallels between fantasy and reality, using real-life situations as elements of idealistic fantasy within Metaphor's world, things that could never happen for the residents of Euchronia.

The way that Metaphor deftly handles so many threads of varying sizes and scopes is nothing short of extraordinary, especially when the themes of anxiety, willpower, and hope are so striking in the real world.

The individual characters in Metaphor are largely brilliantly written, with their own struggles and demons to battle, even if they aren't party members or sometimes even less important to the overarching plot. Characters like the bat-like Eugief, Heismay, battles prejudice for his race whilst trying to mourn the loss of a family member, but displays his own prejudice against the rabbit-like Papirus for their part in his grief. The stoic Brigitta, owner of the capital's magic igniter shop, casts an icy exterior, only to show her true colours through a puppy she can't resist but take in.

Even the game's main antagonist, Louis, is incredibly compelling, bringing an immediate presence to the screen through his striking design and charismatic dialogue, but as is par for the course, there is far more than meets the eye with Louis. There are a lot of plates to spin in Metaphor, but almost all of them are tantalising events to keep returning to.

It's difficult not to make comparisons between Studio Zero's Metaphor and the Persona series, as they share a lot of DNA: a calendar-based system that has you not only exploring dungeons for treasure and boss fights, but also balancing improvement of social stats, or virtues as they're known here, and bonds with party members and other important NPCs to learn more of their personal stories and gain benefits to the more traditional JRPG aspects of the game in the process.

But Metaphor stands alone in Atlus' repertoire by not only drawing from Persona, but the core Shin Megami Tensei franchise as well, while infusing its own flair in its combat mechanics through the Archetype system.

Archetypes in Metaphor are much closer to the Final Fantasy series' job system than they are to Persona, with each character gaining skills and masteries through use of interchangeable classes gained through the social parts of the game, and combat-earned AP gained to level them up. MAG, a currency gained through both combat and socialisation, can be used to transfer skills from one archetype to another, giving you plenty of ways to experiment with your own builds between the seven party members and the 30 different Archetypes alone.

Combat itself utilises the Turn Icon system resembling core Shin Megami Tensei games, with extra actions gained by striking a critical hit or weakness, or serious losses by missing an attack or hitting an absorbed element. You can also strike at an advantage by attacking enemies in the overworld in real-time, or take them out entirely if you're high enough above them in level (a feature I wish all future JRPGs would carry!), but if an enemy gets the jump on you, it can be devastating. All of these elements combine to make arguably the best combat Atlus has ever put out in a JRPG.

It's also impossible to discuss the game without mentioning its frankly stellar soundtrack. Composer Shoji Meguro's unique flavour is felt throughout the game, with one of the best battle themes to ever grace a JRPG, drawing from Buddhist sutra inspirations with priest Keisuke Honryo's unforgettable chanting.

All of this is to say that Metaphor: ReFantazio is an incredible JRPG, and one that will keep you coming back over and over for well over 80 hours. However, on Switch 2, the experience hasn't come across without a few hiccups along the way.

After releasing Persona 3 Reload on Switch 2 last year, which itself suffered greatly from frame rate issues and muddy visuals, and experiencing inconsistencies with Metaphor: ReFantazio itself in its original release, I was sceptical, to say the least, and that scepticism ended up having merit, as I'm not sure I've seen a game look quite so beautiful and hideous at the same time!

From an artistic standpoint, Metaphor is sumptuous, taking the 'more is more' approach from Persona and applying a much wider colour palette; the UI and menus are stunning, of that there's no doubt. But technically speaking, Metaphor is a bit of a mess.

There are options to run in performance, balanced, or graphics modes both handheld and docked, but regardless of which mode you pick, there were inconsistencies throughout. One moment in combat could look stunning AND run well, and the next cutscene you'd encounter the strangest case of anti-aliasing and fuzzy textures, despite there only being two characters in the room.

Frame rate issues also persist regardless of performance or graphics settings, never enough to overtake the other amazing qualities of the game, but always enough to notice and occasionally distract. Persona 3 Reload did eventually get a performance update that fixed a lot of issues; suffice it to say, I truly hope Metaphor gets one as well!

Additionally, while so much of Metaphor's main dungeons and set pieces are a sight to behold, Atlus's dungeon designs have often left something to be desired, and that's apparent in Metaphor's side dungeons. When on a side quest, it's fairly easy to get turned around in the sea of brown tunnels and foggy forest areas, simply on the back of them all looking identical! It's a stark difference to the Regalith Cathedral, Sandworm's Belly, or Skyrunner main dungeons, for sure.

Metaphor: ReFantazio is an extraordinary JRPG, juggling stories and systems alike with grace and effortlessness, but it is let down somewhat by poor optimisation on Switch 2.

Anyone looking for a huge, sprawling adventure with lovable characters, compelling parallels to the real world, and progression systems that will keep you coming back for more will be far from disappointed with this game, but there's no metaphor for a dodgy frame rate and visuals that can occasionally take you out of the fantasy.

Review copy provided by SEGAHow we review productsScoring Policy

A Bowhunt for Caribou Along Alaska’s Famed Haul Road Is Even Tougher Than It Sounds

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 10, 2026 2:58 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

We had to be getting close. I picked my way through the rocks along the edge of the half-frozen creek, knowing that any minute I would intersect trails beaten into the snow by thousands of caribou hooves. But could we make it in time? We couldn’t see the trails as we hurried alongside the steep cut bank, although I knew they had to be right here somewhere. I climbed up a few feet to peek over the bank, only to see a hundred caribou about 50 yards away. They saw me too. The stalk was blown.

The hundreds of caribou that we had tried to ambush were now moving away. Though we could hear the grunting, snorting, and occasional clash of antlers just over the hill, they might as well have been a mile away. They flowed like water over the tundra.

All we could do was look for another group of caribou to stalk. Luckily this shouldn’t be much of a problem, as thousands of caribou were migrating through this snowy stretch of rolling hills and wide valleys at the northern edge of the Brooks Range, within the largest bowhunting-only area on the continent.

The Dalton Highway, or “Haul Road” as it is commonly referred to, could be considered an irreconcilable dichotomy. It’s where ambitious industry and true wilderness meet—and coexist. The 414-mile industrial road was constructed in 1974 to facilitate building the Trans-Alaska Pipeline and service the new oilfields in Prudhoe Bay. It provides the vital link that allows oil to flow all the way to the Port of Valdez to this day.

Although its primary function is still to service the pipeline and oil fields, the Haul Road was opened to the public in 1980 and has provided and created access to millions of acres of public land. The Dalton Highway Corridor Management Area extends five miles on either side of the road and stretches about 370 miles from the Yukon River north to Prudhoe Bay, in which only bowhunting for big game is allowed.

“This is going to be a long drive,” I told my dad and our videographer Tanner Denton as we loaded up our gear with the snow still falling.

With about 500 miles to go and six inches of wet snow on the ground, we had a challenging drive ahead. The Haul Road is not only the most remote highway in the U.S., but it also has a reputation for tough driving conditions. It’s been featured on shows like Ice Road Truckers, and most rental vehicle contracts explicitly forbid driving on it. Even travel guides suggest bringing not one, but two new spare tires. For decades, the road was all gravel, and although some sections are now paved, much of the pavement is honestly worse than the gravel. On the drive, you’ll encounter huge potholes, frost heaves, rocks slung into your windshield from passing big rigs, and a unique silty sludge that will coat your entire vehicle. It was early October, and we knew that wet, muddy, and icy conditions were going to be a fact of life.

Despite the relatively easy access, popularity, and long seasons, I hadn’t ever really given an honest effort to a Haul Road hunt. I’m usually distracted by sheep and moose hunting, and aside from sneaking away on days off while working in Prudhoe Bay a couple times, the last time I had made the drive was in 2009 with my dad. So it was fitting that Dad was coming along with me on this trip.

Even so, this was anything but a slam dunk. As migrators, caribou are where they are, and you find them when you find them.

We hit the road shortly after first light. By the time the sun began to set, we were looking at more than a thousand caribou scattered across the barren hillsides in the northern foothills of the Brooks Range. With the temperature dropping quickly, it was time to set up camp, string our bows, and heat the wood stove. As we discussed our plan for the following morning, we hoped that the caribou tracks that littered the fresh snow around our camp were a good sign. I had been told by folks who are much more experienced than I, that this time of year, the herds of caribou will overnight up in the hills, then begin moving down in mid-morning. It looked like we were in a good position.

The next morning, we began gearing up as the coffee percolator thumped on the small wood stove. We periodically peeked outside the tent, looking for any major movement in the herds of caribou.

Our basic strategy was simple and dictated almost entirely by the terrain and the nature of the caribou. In herds of hundreds or more, on completely barren tundra, it would be all but impossible to sneak close enough for a shot with our recurves. When caribou did pass by terrain features that might allow a stalk, the herds were almost constantly moving. At a feeding, strolling pace, it would be impossible for us to keep up or gain ground on them to get into position. Instead, this hunt had to be an ambush. We had to take advantage of any terrain feature we could hide in, and let the caribou come to us.

“They’re pointed this direction,” I said as I stuck my head out of the tent. “I think we need to get moving.”

A herd of caribou was on its feet and moving on a low ridgeline about a mile away, angling for a ravine a few hundred yards from camp. Before we had much time to react, caribou began popping up onto the ridge on the other side of the creek, having already crossed. We had missed our opportunity.

“I think they’re going to drop down to the bottom eventually, so let’s work around and try to find a way to get in front of them,” I said.

We quickly hooked to our left, just above the base of the hills that the herd was moving along. They didn’t seem to be in a hurry. I don’t often think of caribou as an especially vocal animal, so it’s always surprising to hear their bellowing and snorting. As we crested the top of the hill which had been covered in caribou minutes earlier, we saw the last animals of the herd cresting over another skyline, headed farther back into the mountains. The barnyard-like scent of caribou hung in the air as we walked back.

When I saw the trails converge into one spot crossing the ravine above camp, I knew that this place had everything we needed for an ambush. The caribou had to cross a hilltop, then drop down a tall, steep bank, then cross two different channels before climbing out on a single trail that exited the other side. We would be able to see the next herd coming from camp, then quickly move into position to intercept them.

Back at camp, the percolator hadn’t even started boiling when another herd came over the far ridgeline, headed right for that creek. We threw on our backpacks and grabbed our bows, the herd moving closer with every precious second. The animals were coming from the north and I wanted to be on the south edge of the ravine. We would be able to hide and have plenty of time to see bulls coming and get ready. I didn’t cross the creek soon enough though, and it quickly turned to deep pools covered by unstable ice. Crossing now was too risky, so we continued moving upstream against the steep bank. Just as we had almost reached the trail that the caribou were headed toward, I looked over that cut bank and spookedthe entire herd. It was so close. If we had been just one or two minutes earlier, the outcome might have been different.

By that evening, the herds of caribou had abandoned their ridgetop route and began congregating in and moving through the valley below our camp. After rushing back and forth all day, we jumped in the truck and moved to a spot where we could see the entire valley. As evening began to fall, a big herd that had spent the day in the relative safety of the wide-open and flat valley began to move. Like spilled water trickling off a tabletop, a stream of caribou began to pull from the immense herd. In minutes, the line of caribou stretched as far as we could see into the mountains, crossing the creek just downhill of our camp. We tried one last time to slip into a gap in the herd, but that didn’t work either.

The next morning, we made the short drive from camp to where the road crested a nearby hill, giving us a good vantage of the whole valley. We could see hundreds (if not thousands) of caribou scattered across the foothills in the distance. They were beginning to move down to the valley floor. One cow would make the decision to go, followed by a single-file line of animals until the whole herd vanished. It was apparent that the whole mess of them was going to cross the road in a spot with virtually no cover and a howling 20 mph wind. To complicate matters, two other truckloads of hunters who were camped nearby were already positioning to try and have a crack at the approaching herd. We decided to wait where we were.

It was possible that some caribou could cross the creek we had been hunting, either above or below camp—we just needed to know which way they’d go.

As the caribou flowed across the road, we quickly turned around, parked the truck, and began walking down the creek to intercept them. They were crossing the gulley in a shallow, flat spot, several hundred yards from the edge of any brush. We managed to slip into a sizeable gap in the herd and were frantically trying to find a position to hide and wait, when my dad (who was kneeling right next to me) whispered, “There’s antlers right there, about 30 yards!”

If there’s one thing that’s a dead giveaway that a bull caribou is coming, it’s his set of tall antlers. If there’s any terrain between you, you’ll see his antlers long before he can see you.

If there’s one thing that’s a dead giveaway that a bull caribou is coming, it’s his set of tall antlers. If there’s any terrain between you, you’ll see his antlers long before he can see you. And at that moment, there were antlers sticking up above the small embankment—very close. We were in a bad spot though. I nocked an arrow, but Dad had to move away from me slightly. When he did that, the caribou spotted us and spooked, circling wide and crossing the shallow depression.

We cautiously stood and, not seeing any more caribou, tried to find a better position. Soon we spotted more antlers over a softly rolling rise in the tundra. Down on my knees, arrow nocked, I knew they were going to cross too far from us. In good conditions, 40 yards was pushing my reasonable limit with my recurve, but with a 20-mph wind, it was out of the question. One herd crossed at 75 yards, then another at 60, and then the herd fizzled out. After repositioning again and waiting, we decided to grab a cup of coffee and regroup. Each time we were getting closer, and we knew that something had to break our way sooner or later.

Before a much-needed lunch, I opted to drive back up to our vantage point and make sure nothing else was coming. Almost as soon as we got there, Dad and I saw yet another big herd. This group was on a trajectory that would take them near the line of tall brush that lined much of the creek, and we might have a place to hide. Again, we parked and hurried, walking through the brush as quickly as we could.

“We’re not going to make it in time,” I said, frustrated by our constant race against the clock.

I could see the backs of the caribou through the brush as they made their way across the open flat. They were just too fast, and we came to the end of the brush just as they started crossing the creek about 120 yards away.

“Why don’t you just try sneaking up there by yourself,” my dad said. “Three guys are too many, but one might be able to slip in there.”

It was worth a shot. Between us and the herd, the wash that the creek flowed through was about 3 to 4 feet deep, with a little bit of rolling terrain that might obscure the caribou that were coming. After a hundred or so had crossed, I began quickly, but carefully, moving down the draw. Eventually, some of the caribou that had already crossed began to see me, but kept on their way. I was still obscured from most of the animals that hadn’t yet crossed. I was skeptical about my chances, but after a small group saw me at about 60 yards, flared, and crossed farther down, a gap in the herd appeared. With antlers just coming into view, I quickly covered the remaining distance and dropped to my knees. I hid next to a small bush, arms-length from the main trail the herd had created. I watched the antlers, which looked to be following the group I had just bumped. There was nothing I could do but wait. I quickly ranged a rock in the cut bank across the winding trails in front of me. It was 30 yards away. I told myself I wouldn’t shoot at anything beyond that.

Seeing the rest of the herd safely across the creek must have calmed the caribou, because they quickly turned back toward me. All I could think as they closed the distance was: Don’t make eye contact.

Exposed, but holding absolutely still with my head tilted down, the cow that was leading the group came closer, followed by a nice bull. At under 10 yards I could hear her sniffing the air, even over the whipping wind. The bull was beyond her. As if I didn’t even exist, the cow walked past me, only paces away. My eyes shifted to the bull who was quartering to me, then to the rock I had ranged. He was only about 20-25 yards I figured, so I drew my bow.

I was in a state of calm disbelief. Somehow I accepted that, aside from the timing and placement of my arrow, the situation was totally out of my control. I expected the bull to come out of his skin as soon as I moved, but he didn’t even notice me until I hit full draw. His head turned toward me at some point, but I only remember checking off my shot process. Anchor, good alignment, aim, expand—and the arrow disappeared into the bull. I wanted to hit him through the meat of the front shoulder to fully catch both lungs, but my arrow went a little high and left. The bull crossed the creek in a sprint then turned back. I stood to watch him as he ran about 150 yards back to meet the confused herd. The second the bull stopped running, another big bull ran up, hooked his antlers, and threw my bull on the ground, thrashing him as if my bull owed him money.

After the herd moved off, I walked up to my bull, still in disbelief. This beautiful white-caped bull at my feet was the true embodiment of the Arctic. As a hunter, I always experience a sense of thankfulness and relief when I can finally put my hands on an animal that I’ve hunted, and I felt that with this bull.

But more than the thankfulness for possession, of meat, hide, antlers, there was something that I haven’t experienced so strongly in a long time. It was a raw appreciation of that moment, that every hunter feels right when it works out. The feeling transcends space and time and connects the hunter to the feeling of the arrow being released. That experience is a true gift that will last long after all the meat is gone.

Tyler Freel is a Staff Writer for Outdoor Life. He lives in Fairbanks, Alaska and has been covering a variety of topics for OL for more than a decade. From backpack sheep hunting adventure stories to DIY tips to gear and gun reviews, he covers it all with a perspective that’s based in experience.
